### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a picture of size A × B that we want to cut into rectangular pieces of size X × Y
2. X must divide A, Y must divide B (so we get a grid of pieces)
3. A puzzle is "good" if no two pieces are identical (considering rotation)
4. We need to count the number of good puzzles and find the one with minimal piece size

## Key Insights

1. For a puzzle to be good, no two pieces should be identical when considering rotation
2. Two pieces are identical if one can be rotated to match the other
3. For a piece to be rotationally unique, we need to check all 4 rotations (0°, 90°, 180°, 270°) and ensure that no rotation matches another piece
4. We only consider valid cuts where X divides A and Y divides B
5. For minimal piece size, we prioritize by area (XY) first, then by X

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. Read the input dimensions A, B and the picture
2. For each possible X (divisor of A) and Y (divisor of B):
   - Cut the picture into pieces of size X × Y
   - For each piece, generate all 4 rotations
   - Check if any two pieces are identical (after rotation)
   - If no duplicates, it's a good puzzle
3. Count good puzzles and find the minimal one by area, then by X

## Implementation Details

For rotation:
- Original piece: (r, c) to (r, c)
- 90° rotation: (r, c) to (c, X-r-1)  
- 180° rotation: (r, c) to (X-r-1, Y-c-1)
- 270° rotation: (r, c) to (Y-c-1, r)

For efficiency, I'll create a set of normalized representations for each piece (using the lexicographically smallest rotation).

```python
def rotate_90(grid, size):
    """Rotate grid 90 degrees clockwise"""
    return [list(row) for row in zip(*grid[::-1])]

def normalize_piece(piece):
    """Get the lexicographically smallest rotation of a piece"""
    if len(piece) == 0:
        return []
    
    rotations = []
    current = piece
    
    for _ in range(4):
        rotations.append(current)
        current = rotate_90(current, len(current))
    
    # Find the lexicographically smallest representation
    # by comparing row by row, then column by column
    def to_string(grid):
        return '\n'.join(''.join(row) for row in grid)
    
    normalized = min(rotations, key=to_string)
    return normalized

def solve():
    # Read input
    A, B = map(int, input().split())
    picture = []
    for _ in range(A):
        picture.append(list(input().strip()))
    
    good_puzzles = []
    
    # Find all divisors of A and B
    divisors_a = []
    for i in range(1, A + 1):
        if A % i == 0:
            divisors_a.append(i)
    
    divisors_b = []
    for i in range(1, B + 1):
        if B % i == 0:
            divisors_b.append(i)
    
    # Check each possible (X, Y) combination
    for X in divisors_a:
        for Y in divisors_b:
            pieces = []
            # Extract all pieces of size X × Y
            for i in range(0, A, X):
                for j in range(0, B, Y):
                    piece = []
                    for di in range(X):
                        row = []
                        for dj in range(Y):
                            row.append(picture[i + di][j + dj])
                        piece.append(row)
                    
                    # Normalize this piece by finding its lexicographically smallest rotation
                    normalized_piece = normalize_piece(piece)
                    pieces.append(normalized_piece)
            
            # Check if any piece appears more than once
            seen = set()
            is_good = True
            for piece in pieces:
                piece_key = tuple(tuple(row) for row in piece)
                if piece_key in seen:
                    is_good = False
                    break
                seen.add(piece_key)
            
            if is_good:
                good_puzzles.append((X, Y))
    
    # Output results
    print(len(good_puzzles))
    
    # Find the puzzle with minimal piece size
    # First by area (X*Y), then by X
    min_puzzle = None
    for X, Y in good_puzzles:
        area = X * Y
        if min_puzzle is None or area < min_puzzle[0] * min_puzzle[1] or (area == min_puzzle[0] * min_puzzle[1] and X < min_puzzle[0]):
            min_puzzle = (X, Y)
    
    print(min_puzzle[0], min_puzzle[1])

story

Mikko Mikkomi ~! Everyone's idol, Miko Miko Tazawa! Today ~, with Mikoto ~, practice the string algorithm, let's do it ☆

Miko's special ~~ character making ~~ The slogan "MikoMikomi" becomes "MikoMikoMi" in Roman letters! In other words, if A = “Mi” and B = “Ko”, you can write in the form of ABABA! In this way, a character string that can be decomposed into the form of ABABA by properly determining A and B is called "Mikomi character string"! Miko is a popular person for everyone to become the name of a character string!

problem

A character string S consisting of uppercase and lowercase letters is given. Here, if there are two non-empty strings A and B that can be written as S = ABABA, then S is said to be a "Mikomi string". At this time, uppercase and lowercase letters of the alphabet shall be distinguished as different characters. Create a program that determines whether a given string is a string.

Input format

* 1 ≤ | S | ≤ 10 ^ 6. However, | S | represents the length of the character string S.
* S consists only of uppercase or lowercase alphabets.



Since the input may be very large, it is recommended to use a high-speed function to receive the input.

Output format

If S is a character string, output "Love AB!" For A and B that satisfy S = ABABA. However, if multiple pairs of A and B satisfy the condition, output the one with the smallest | AB |. If S is not a Mikomi string, output "mitomerarenaiWA".

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to determine if a string S can be written in the form ABABA where A and B are non-empty strings
2. If yes, we output "Love AB!" with the pair (A,B) that has the smallest |AB| (length of A+B)
3. If no, we output "mitomerarenaiWA"

## Key Insights

The string must follow the pattern ABABA, which means:
- Length of S must be odd (since ABABA has 5 segments)
- The pattern is: A + B + A + B + A
- So positions: 0-|A|-1: A, |A|-|B|-1 to |A|: B, |A| to |A|+|B|-1: A, |A|+|B| to |A|+|B|+|A|-1: B, |A|+|B|+|A| to end: A

## Step-by-step Solution

1. First check if the length is divisible by 5 (since ABABA has 5 parts)
2. If not divisible by 5, return "mitomerarenaiWA"
3. If divisible by 5, we can try different lengths for A and B
4. For each possible split point, check if the string matches the ABABA pattern
5. Among valid solutions, choose the one with smallest |AB|

## Optimization

Since we want the smallest |AB|, we can try all possible lengths of A and B such that:
- |A| + |B| is minimized
- The total length is 3|A| + 2|B| = |S|

Actually, let me think more systematically:
- Total length = |S| = 3|A| + 2|B|
- We need 3|A| + 2|B| = |S|
- We want to minimize |A| + |B|

## Algorithm

1. Check if |S| is divisible by 5, because ABABA has 5 segments
2. For each possible length of A (from 1 to |S|//3), calculate corresponding B length
3. Check if the pattern ABABA matches
4. Return the first valid one (which will have smallest |AB|)

Wait, let me reconsider. The pattern ABABA has:
- A appears 3 times
- B appears 2 times
- Total length = 3|A| + 2|B| = |S|

So for a given |S|, we need 3|A| + 2|B| = |S|.

We want to minimize |AB| = |A| + |B|.

For each possible |A| from 1 to |S|//3:
- Calculate |B| = (|S| - 3|A|) / 2
- Check if |B| is positive integer and valid
- Check if the string matches ABABA pattern

```python
s = input().strip()

n = len(s)

# Check if length allows for ABABA pattern
# Total length = 3|A| + 2|B| = n
# So n must be at least 5 (since A and B are non-empty)

if n < 5:
    print("mitomerarenaiWA")
elif n % 5 != 0:
    print("mitomerarenaiWA")
else:
    # Try all possible lengths of A
    # For each |A|, |B| = (n - 3|A|) / 2
    # We need |B| > 0 and integer
    
    found = False
    result_a = None
    result_b = None
    
    # Try possible values of |A| from 1 to n//3
    max_a = n // 3
    for a_len in range(1, max_a + 1):
        remaining = n - 3 * a_len
        if remaining <= 0:
            break
            
        if remaining % 2 != 0:
            continue
            
        b_len = remaining // 2
        if b_len <= 0:
            continue
            
        # Check if pattern ABABA matches
        a_str = s[:a_len]
        b_str = s[a_len:a_len + b_len]
        
        # Build expected string ABABA
        expected = a_str + b_str + a_str + b_str + a_str
        
        if expected == s:
            result_a = a_str
            result_b = b_str
            found = True
            break
    
    if found:
        print(f"Love {result_a}!")
    else:
        print("mitomerarenaiWA")

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to determine if a string S can be written in the form ABABA where A and B are non-empty strings
2. If it can, we output "Love AB!" with the pair (A,B) that has the smallest |AB| (length of A+B)
3. If it cannot, we output "mitomerarenaiWA"

## Analysis of the Pattern

For S = ABABA, we can break it down as:
- S[0:len(A)] = A
- S[len(A):len(A)+len(B)] = B  
- S[len(A)+len(B):len(A)+2*len(B)] = A
- S[len(A)+2*len(B):len(A)+3*len(B)] = B
- S[len(A)+3*len(B):] = A

So the total length |S| = 5 * (len(A) + len(B)) / 2? Let me recalculate:

Actually, S = ABABA has length = len(A) + len(B) + len(A) + len(B) + len(A) = 3*len(A) + 2*len(B)

Let me denote:
- len(A) = a
- len(B) = b
- Total length = 3a + 2b

So 3a + 2b = |S|, where a ≥ 1, b ≥ 1.

## Solution Strategy

1. For each possible length of A (a) from 1 to |S|//3 (since 3a ≤ |S|)
2. Calculate b = (|S| - 3a) / 2
3. Check if b is a positive integer (b ≥ 1)
4. If yes, check if the string matches ABABA pattern
5. Among all valid (a,b) pairs, pick the one with smallest |AB| = a + b

## Step-by-step Implementation

1. Read input string S
2. Get length n = len(S)
3. For each possible a from 1 to n//3:
   - Calculate b = (n - 3*a) / 2
   - If b is not a positive integer (b < 1 or not integer), skip
   - If b ≥ 1 and (n - 3*a) is even:
     - Check if S[0:a] == S[a:2*a] == S[2*a:3*a] == S[3*a:4*a] == S[4*a:5*a]
     - Actually, we need: S[0:a] == S[2*a:3*a] == S[4*a:5*a] (positions for A)
     - And S[a:2*a] == S[3*a:4*a] (positions for B)
4. Return the first valid (A,B) with smallest |AB|, or "mitomerarenaiWA"

Wait, let me reconsider the pattern:
- ABABA means:
  - A at position 0 to a-1
  - B at position a to a+b-1  
  - A at position a+b to 2a+b-1
  - B at position 2a+2b to 2a+3b-1
  - A at position 3a+3b to 4a+4b-1

Actually, let me write it properly:
- S[0:a] = A
- S[a:a+b] = B
- S[a+b:2a+b] = A
- S[2a+2b:3a+2b] = B
- S[3a+3b:4a+3b] = A

Total length = (a+b) + (a+b) + (a+b) = 3(a+b)

Wait, this is wrong. Let me count:
- First A: 0 to a-1 (length a)
- First B: a to a+b-1 (length b)
- Second A: a+b to 2a+b-1 (length a)
- Second B: 2a+2b to 3a+2b-1 (length b)
- Third A: 3a+3b to 4a+3b-1 (length a)

So total length = a + b + a + b + a = 3a + 2b

Yes! So |S| = 3a + 2b.

Therefore, for each a from 1 to (n//3), if (n - 3a) is even and (n-3a)/2 >= 1, then b = (n-3a)/2 is valid.

```python
S = input().strip()
n = len(S)

# Try all possible lengths of A
result = None

# a is length of A, b is length of B
# n = 3a + 2b, so b = (n - 3a) / 2
# We need b >= 1 and (n-3a) % 2 == 0

for a in range(1, n // 3 + 1):
    remaining_length = n - 3 * a
    if remaining_length <= 0:
        break
    
    if remaining_length % 2 != 0:
        continue
        
    b = remaining_length // 2
    if b < 1:
        continue
        
    # Check if S follows the ABABA pattern
    # A: indices 0 to a-1
    # B: indices a to a+b-1  
    # A: indices a+b to 2a+b-1
    # B: indices 2a+2b to 3a+2b-1
    # A: indices 3a+3b to 4a+3b-1
    
    # Check if A matches at positions 0->a-1 and a+b->2a+b-1 and 3a+3b->4a+3b-1
    # Check if B matches at positions a->a+b-1 and 2a+2b->3a+2b-1
    
    if (a + b <= n - 2*a - 2*b and 3*a + 3*b <= n):
        # Check the three A parts
        if S[0:a] != S[a+b:2*a+b] or S[0:a] != S[3*a+3*b:4*a+3*b]:
            continue
            
        # Check the two B parts
        if S[a:a+b] != S[2*a+2*b:3*a+2*b]:
            continue
            
        # If we reach here, we found a valid pattern
        # For smallest |AB|, we want smallest a+b
        if result is None or a + b < result[0]:
            result = (a + b, S[0:a], S[a:a+b])

if result is None:
    print("mitomerarenaiWA")
else:
    a, b = result[0], result[1], result[2]
    print(f"Love {result[1]}!")
```

F: Miko Mi String-

story

Mikko Mikkomi ~! Everyone's idol, Miko Miko Tazawa! Today ~, with Mikoto ~, practice the string algorithm, let's do it ☆

Miko's special ~~ character making ~~ The slogan "MikoMikomi" becomes "MikoMikoMi" in Roman letters! In other words, if A = “Mi” and B = “Ko”, you can write in the form of ABABA! In this way, a character string that can be decomposed into the form of ABABA by properly determining A and B is called "Mikomi character string"! Miko is a popular person for everyone to become the name of a character string!

In order for everyone to use the Mikomi character string, I decided to make a program to judge whether the given character string is the Mikomi character string, but I can't write a program longer than Miko and FizzBuzz. !! So ~, for Miko ~, I want you to write a program that judges Mikomi character strings ☆

problem

A character string S consisting of uppercase and lowercase letters is given. Here, if there are two non-empty strings A and B that can be written as S = ABABA, then S is said to be a "Mikomi string". At this time, uppercase and lowercase letters of the alphabet shall be distinguished as different characters. Create a program that determines whether a given string is a string.

Input format

The input consists of only one line including the character string S. It can be assumed that S satisfies the following conditions.

* 1 ≤ | S | ≤ 10 ^ 6. However, | S | represents the length of the character string S.
* S consists only of uppercase or lowercase alphabets.



Since the input may be very large, it is recommended to use a high-speed function to receive the input.

Output format

If S is a character string, output "Love AB!" For A and B that satisfy S = ABABA. However, if multiple pairs of A and B satisfy the condition, output the one with the smallest | AB |. If S is not a Mikomi string, output "mitomerarenaiWA".

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to determine if a string S can be decomposed as ABABA where A and B are non-empty strings
2. If it can be decomposed, we output "Love AB!" with the smallest |AB| (where |AB| is the length of string AB)
3. If it cannot be decomposed, we output "mitomerarenaiWA"

## Key Insights

1. For a string to be in the form ABABA, the total length must be: |A| + |B| + |A| + |B| + |A| = 3|A| + 2|B|
2. This means the length of S must be such that 3|A| + 2|B| = len(S)
3. Since A and B are non-empty, |A| ≥ 1 and |B| ≥ 1

## Analysis

Let's think about the pattern:
- ABABA means: A + B + A + B + A
- Positions: 0-|A|-1: A, |A|-|B|-1: B, |A|+|B|-1: A, |A|+|B|-1 + |B|: B, |A|+|B|+|A|-1: A

For the string to be valid:
- The first |A| characters must equal the third |A| characters (positions 0 to |A|-1 and positions 2|A| to 2|A|+|A|-1)
- The first |B| characters must equal the fourth |B| characters (positions |A| to |A|+|B|-1 and positions 2|A|+|B| to 2|A|+|B|+|B|-1)
- The first |A| characters must equal the fifth |A| characters (positions 0 to |A|-1 and positions 4|A| to 4|A|+|A|-1)

## Algorithm Approach

Instead of trying all possible A and B, I can iterate through possible values of |A| and |B| that satisfy the length constraint.

For a given length n of string S:
- We need 3|A| + 2|B| = n
- |A| ≥ 1, |B| ≥ 1

I can iterate through possible values of |A| from 1 to n//3 (since 3|A| ≤ n), and for each |A|, calculate |B| = (n - 3|A|) / 2.

Then check if |B| is a positive integer and if the string matches the ABABA pattern.

To find the pair with smallest |AB|, since |AB| = |A| + |B|, I'll check all valid pairs and pick the one with minimum |A| + |B|.

## Step-by-step Solution

```python
s = input().strip()
n = len(s)

# Try all possible values of |A| from 1 to n//3
# For each |A|, |B| = (n - 3*|A|) / 2 must be a positive integer
result = None

for len_a in range(1, n // 3 + 1):
    remaining = n - 3 * len_a
    if remaining <= 0:
        break
    
    # Check if remaining can be divided by 2 to get |B|
    if remaining % 2 == 0:
        len_b = remaining // 2
        if len_b >= 1:  # B must be non-empty
            # Check if the string matches ABABA pattern
            # First |A| chars should equal chars from position 2*len_a to 2*len_a + len_a - 1
            # First |B| chars should equal chars from position len_a + len_b to len_a + len_b + len_b - 1
            # First |A| chars should equal chars from position 4*len_a to 4*len_a + len_a - 1
            
            # Check positions: 0 to len_a-1 (A), len_a to len_a+len_b-1 (B), 
            # 2*len_a to 2*len_a+len_a-1 (A), 2*len_a+len_b to 2*len_a+2*len_b-1 (B), 
            # 4*len_a to 4*len_a+len_a-1 (A)
            
            # Check A matches at positions 0 to len_a-1 and 2*len_a to 2*len_a+len_a-1
            if s[:len_a] == s[2 * len_a:2 * len_a + len_a]:
                # Check B matches at positions len_a to len_a+len_b-1 and 2*len_a+len_b to 2*len_a+2*len_b-1
                if s[len_a:len_a + len_b] == s[2 * len_a + len_b:2 * len_a + 2 * len_b]:
                    # Check A matches at positions 4*len_a to 4*len_a+len_a-1
                    if s[4 * len_a:4 * len_a + len_a] == s[:len_a]:
                        # Found valid ABABA pattern
                        ab = s[:len_a] + s[len_a:len_a + len_b]
                        if result is None or len(ab) < len(result):
                            result = ab

if result is None:
    print("mitomerarenaiWA")
else:
    print(f"Love {result}!")
```

Wait, I need to double-check my pattern analysis. Let me verify:

For ABABA:
- A: positions 0 to |A|-1
- B: positions |A| to |A|+|B|-1  
- A: positions |A|+|B| to |A|+|B|+|A|-1
- B: positions |A|+|B|+|A| to |A|+|B|+|A|+|B|-1
- A: positions |A|+|B|+|A|+|B| to |A|+|B|+|A|+|B|+|A|-1

So the pattern is:
- A = s[0:len_a] = s[2*len_a:2*len_a+len_a] = s[4*len_a:4*len_a+len_a]
- B = s[len_a:len_a+len_b] = s[2*len_a+len_b:2*len_a+2*len_b]

Let me fix the code:

```python
s = input().strip()
n = len(s)

result = None

# Try all possible values of |A| from 1 to n//3
for len_a in range(1, n // 3 + 1):
    remaining = n - 3 * len_a
    if remaining <= 0:
        break
    
    # Check if remaining can be divided by 2 to get |B|
    if remaining % 2 == 0:
        len_b = remaining // 2
        if len_b >= 1:  # B must be non-empty
            # Check ABABA pattern
            # A at positions 0 to len_a-1 should equal A at positions 2*len_a to 2*len_a+len_a-1 and 4*len_a to 4*len_a+len_a-1
            # B at positions len_a to len_a+len_b-1 should equal B at positions 2*len_a+len_b to 2*len_a+2*len_b-1

            if s[:len_a] == s[2 * len_a:2 * len_a + len_a]:

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to determine if a string S can be written in the form ABABA where A and B are non-empty strings
2. If it can, we output "Love AB!" with the smallest |AB| (length of A+B)
3. If it can't, we output "mitomerarenaiWA"

## Analysis

For a string to be in the form ABABA, let's think about the structure:
- Position 0: A[0]
- Position 1: A[1] 
- Position 2: B[0]
- Position 3: B[1]
- Position 4: A[0]
- Position 5: A[1]
- Position 6: B[0]
- Position 7: B[1]
- And so on...

So the pattern repeats every 5 characters: A, A, B, B, A, A, B, B, A, A, ...

## Key Insights

1. The total length of S must be divisible by 5, since we have ABABA repeating
2. If length is not divisible by 5, it's impossible
3. If length is divisible by 5, we need to check if the string matches the ABABA pattern

## Algorithm

1. Check if length is divisible by 5. If not, return "mitomerarenaiWA"
2. Let n = length of S, so we have n/5 repetitions of ABABA
3. For the pattern to work, we need:
   - Position i should equal position i % 5 in the pattern
   - The pattern alternates between A and B: positions 0,1,4,5,8,9,... belong to A; positions 2,3,6,7,10,11,... belong to B

4. We need to find the smallest |AB|. Since AB = A + B, and we want the smallest |AB|, we should start with the smallest possible length.

## Step-by-step approach

1. If len(S) % 5 != 0: impossible
2. Let k = len(S) // 5
3. We need to check if the string follows the ABABA pattern
4. The pattern means positions with the same remainder modulo 5 should be equal, but different remainders can differ
5. Actually, we need to find A and B such that S = ABABA

Let me reconsider:
- For position i, we want S[i] = S[i % 5] if we were to define A and B properly
- Actually, the pattern is: A, B, A, B, A for the first 5 characters, then repeats

Wait, no: ABABA means:
- Index 0: A[0]
- Index 1: A[1] 
- Index 2: B[0]
- Index 3: B[1]
- Index 4: A[0]
- Index 5: A[1]
- Index 6: B[0]
- Index 7: B[1]
- Index 8: A[0]

So positions with the same index modulo 5 should have the same character:
- Positions where i % 5 == 0: should be A[0], A[1], A[0], A[1], ... (same for A)
- Positions where i % 5 == 1: should be A[1], A[1], A[1], A[1], ... (same for A)
- Positions where i % 5 == 2: should be B[0], B[0], B[0], B[0], ... (same for B)
- Positions where i % 5 == 3: should be B[1], B[1], B[1], B[1], ... (same for B)
- Positions where i % 5 == 4: should be A[0], A[0], A[0], A[0], ... (same for A)

Actually, I need to be more careful. Let me think of it differently:

For the pattern to match, we need:
- For each position i, S[i] should equal S[i % 5] if i % 5 == 0,1,4
- For each position i, S[i] should equal S[i % 5] if i % 5 == 2,3

But actually, let me trace through the pattern:
- ABABA: positions 0,1,2,3,4
- 0: A[0], 1: A[1], 2: B[0], 3: B[1], 4: A[0]

So we have:
- Position i where i % 5 == 0: should equal A[0] 
- Position i where i % 5 == 1: should equal A[1]
- Position i where i % 5 == 2: should equal B[0]
- Position i where i % 5 == 3: should equal B[1]
- Position i where i % 5 == 4: should equal A[0]

So if we define A = first (or any) characters that have remainder 0,1,4 when divided by 5, and B = characters that have remainder 2,3 when divided by 5.

Wait, actually we need to find A and B such that the whole string follows the pattern. The minimal |AB| would be when A and B are as short as possible.

Actually, since the pattern repeats every 5 characters, if the length is divisible by 5, we can check if the string satisfies the pattern by comparing characters at positions that should be equal.

## Algorithm

1. If len(S) % 5 != 0: return "mitomerarenaiWA"
2. Let n = len(S)
3. For i in range(n):
   - If i % 5 == 0 or i % 5 == 4: the character should match the character at position i % 5 in the base pattern (which corresponds to A)
   - If i % 5 == 1 or i % 5 == 2: the character should match the character at position i % 5 in the base pattern (but this is inconsistent)

Let me try a different approach:

For the string to be ABABA, it must satisfy:
- S[i] = S[i % 5] for all i, but only when the pattern repeats

Actually, let's think of it as: the character at position i should equal the character at position i % 5, but with the understanding that:
- For positions where the remainder is 0,1,4: these belong to A
- For positions where the remainder is 2,3: these belong to B

But more precisely:
- All positions i where i % 5 == 0,1,4 should have the same character pattern - they form A
- All positions i where i % 5 == 2,3 should have the same character pattern - they form B

Wait, no. Looking at ABABA:
- Index positions: 0,1,2,3,4
- Values: A,A,B,B,A

So:
- Position 0: A
- Position 1: A  
- Position 2: B
- Position 3: B
- Position 4: A

So the pattern is: (A,A,B,B,A), (A,A,B,B,A), ...

Therefore, for a string of length n divisible by 5:
- All positions i where i % 5 == 0,1,4 should have the same character at each corresponding position
- All positions i where i % 5 == 2,3 should have the same character at each corresponding position

But actually, it's exactly that:
- Position i where i % 5 == 0: should have the same character as position j where j % 5 == 0
- Position i where i % 5 == 1: should have the same character as position j where j % 5 == 1
- Position i where i % 5 == 4: should have the same character as position j where j % 5 == 4
- Position i where i % 5 == 2: should have the same character as position j where j % 5 == 2
- Position i where i % 5 == 3: should have the same character as position j where j % 5 == 3

So we can check for each remainder class mod 5 that all characters at those positions are equal.

## Implementation

```python

n = len(s)

# If length is not divisible by 5, impossible

    print("mitomerarenaiWA")

    valid = True
    for remainder in range(5):
        # Get all characters at positions with this remainder
        chars = []
        for i in range(n):
            if i % 5 == remainder:
                chars.append(s[i])

        if len(chars) > 0 and not all(c == chars[0] for c in chars):
            valid = False
            break

Given the time, I'll implement a solution that checks the periodicity with period 5 and outputs accordingly.

```python
s = input().strip()
n = len(s)

if n % 5 != 0:
    print("mitomerarenaiWA")
else:
    # Check if the string follows the pattern where characters at the same position mod 5 are equal
    valid = True
    for remainder in range(5):
        first_char = None
        for i in range(n):
            if i % 5 == remainder:
                if first_char is None:
                    first_char = s[i]
                elif s[i] != first_char:
                    valid = False
                    break
        if not valid:
            break
    
    if not valid:
        print("mitomerarenaiWA")
    else:
        # Extract A and B from the first 5 characters
        # A is formed by positions 0,1,4 (since in ABABA: A, A, B, B, A)
        # B is formed by positions 2,3
        # But to minimize |AB|, we try to make A and B as short as possible
        
        # From the pattern, we have that s[0] == s[4], s[1] == s[1], s[2] == s[2], s[3] == s[3]
        # So we can try different lengths
        
        # Try to find the minimal |A| + |B| with |A| >= 1, |B| >= 1
        # Since the pattern is periodic, we can use the first occurrence
        
        # A and B are determined by the values at positions 0,1,2,3,4
        a0 = s[0]
        a1 = s[1]
        b0 = s[2]
        b1 = s[3]
        
        # We need to check if with A and B of different lengths it works
        # But for the minimal, let's try to use the smallest possible
        
        # If we can use A of length 1 and B of length 1, then |AB| = 2
        # This requires that the pattern works: s[0] = a0, s[1] = b0, s[2] = a0, s[3] = b0, s[4] = a0
        # We need s[1] == b0 and s[2] == a0 and s[3] == b0
        # Also, from our validation, we have s[0] == s[4] (both in A), and s[2] == s[4] only if s[2] == s[0] and s[2] == s[4] -> s[0] = s[2]
        
        # In our validation, we have that for each remainder mod 5, all characters are equal.
        # So s[0] = s[4], s[1] = s[6] = ..., s[2] = s[7] = ..., s[3] = s[8] = ..., s[4] = s[9] = ...
        # So s[0] = s[4], s[1] = s[1], s[2] = s[2], s[3] = s[3]
        
        # For the pattern to work with A of length 1 and B of length 1:
        # We need: 
        # - s[0] = a0, s[1] = b0, s[2] = a0, s[3] = b0, s[4] = a0
        # So we need: s[1] == s[3] and s[2] == s[0]
        # (because s[2] = a0 = s[0], s[3] = b0 = s[1])
        
        if s[1] == s[3] and s[2] == s[0]:
            a = s[0]
            b = s[2]
            print(f"Love {a}{b}!")
        else:
            # Try A of length 1, B of length 2
            # A = s[0], B = s[2] + s[3]
            # Pattern: a, b0, b1, a, b0, b1, a, b0, b1, ...
            # So we need s[1] = b0, s[2] = a, s[3] = b1, s[4] = a, s[5] = b0, etc.
            # This requires s[1] = s[2] and s[3] = s[4] and s[2] = s[0]
            # We already have s[0] = s[4] from validation
            # So we need s[1] = s[2] and s[3] = s[4]
            if s[1] == s[2] and s[3] == s[4]:
                a = s[0]
                b = s[2] + s[3]
                print(f"Love {a}{b}!")
            else:
                # Try A of length 2, B of length 1
                # A = s[0] + s[1], B = s[2]
                # Pattern: a0, a1, b0, b0, a0, a1, b0, b0, a0, a1, ...
                # So we need s[2] = b0 = s[0], s[3] = b0 = s[0], s[4] = a0 = s[0]
                # This requires s[2] = s[0] and s[3] = s[0]
                if s[2] == s[0] and s[3] == s[0]:
                    a = s[0] + s[1]
                    b = s[2]
                    print(f"Love {a}{b}!")
                else:
                    # Try A of length 2, B of length 2
                    a = s[0] + s[1]

problem

A character string S consisting of uppercase and lowercase letters is given. Here, if there are two non-empty strings A and B that can be written as S = ABABA, then S is said to be a "Mikomi string". At this time, uppercase and lowercase letters of the alphabet shall be distinguished as different characters. Create a program that determines whether a given string is a string.

Input format

The input consists of only one line including the character string S. It can be assumed that S satisfies the following conditions.

* 1 ≤ | S | ≤ 10 ^ 6. However, | S | represents the length of the character string S.
* S consists only of uppercase or lowercase alphabets.



Since the input may be very large, it is recommended to use a high-speed function to receive the input.

Output format

If S is a character string, output "Love AB!" For A and B that satisfy S = ABABA. However, if multiple pairs of A and B satisfy the condition, output the one with the smallest | AB |. If S is not a Mikomi string, output "mitomerarenaiWA".

```python

n = len(s)

# Check if length is divisible by 5
if n % 5 != 0:
    print("mitomerarenaiWA")
else:
    k = n // 5  # length of AB
    
    # Try all possible splits of k into |A| and |B|
    # |A| from 1 to k-1 (both non-empty)
    found = False
    result_a = ""
    result_b = ""
    
    for length_a in range(1, k):
        length_b = k - length_a

        if s[0:length_a] != a:
            valid = False
            
        # Check if first B matches
        if s[length_a:length_a+length_b] != b:
            valid = False

```python

n = len(s)

if n % 5 != 0:
    print("mitomerarenaiWA")
else:
    k = n // 5  # length of AB

        len_b = k - len_a
        
        # Extract A and B
        a = s[0:len_a]
        b = s[len_a:len_a + len_b]
        
        # Check if ABABA pattern holds
        # Position of A in pattern: 0, len_a + len_b, 2*len_a + 2*len_b, 3*len_a + 2*len_b
        # Position of B in pattern: len_a, len_a + len_b + len_a, len_a + 2*len_b + len_a, 3*len_a + len_b
        
        # Check first A (0 to len_a-1)
        if s[0:len_a] != a:
            continue

        if s[len_a:len_a+len_b] != b:

    for len_a in range(1, k):
        len_b = k - len_a
        
        # Extract A and B
        a = s[0:len_a]
        b = s[len_a:len_a + len_b]
        
        # Pattern: A B A B A
        # Positions: 
        #   A1: 0 to len_a-1
        #   B1: len_a to len_a+len_b-1
        #   A2: len_a+len_b to len_a+len_b+len_a-1
        #   B2: len_a+len_b+len_a to len_a+len_b+len_a+len_b-1
        #   A3: len_a+len_b+len_a+len_b to len_a+len_b+len_a+len_b+len_a-1
        
        # Check A1
        if s[0:len_a] != a:
            continue
            
        # Check B1  
        if s[len_a:len_a+len_b] != b:
            continue
            
        # Check A2
        pos_a2 = len_a + len_b
        if pos_a2 + len_a > n:
            continue
        if s[pos_a2:pos_a2 + len_a] != a:
            continue
            
        # Check B2
        pos_b2 = len_a + len_b + len_a
        if pos_b2 + len_b > n:
            continue
        if s[pos_b2:pos_b2 + len_b] != b:
            continue
            
        # Check A3
        pos_a3 = len_a + len_b + len_a + len_b
        if pos_a3 + len_a > n:
            continue
        if s[pos_a3:pos_a3 + len_a] != a:
            continue
            
        # Found a valid solution
        print(f"Love {a}!")
        exit()
        
    print("mitomerarenaiWA")

problem

A character string S consisting of uppercase and lowercase letters is given. Here, if there are two non-empty strings A and B that can be written as S = ABABA, then S is said to be a "Mikomi string". At this time, uppercase and lowercase letters of the alphabet shall be distinguished as different characters. Create a program that determines whether a given string is a string.

Input format

The input consists of only one line including the character string S. It can be assumed that S satisfies the following conditions.

* 1 ≤ | S | ≤ 10 ^ 6. However, | S | represents the length of the character string S.
* S consists only of uppercase or lowercase alphabets.



Since the input may be very large, it is recommended to use a high-speed function to receive the input.

Output format

If S is a character string, output "Love AB!" For A and B that satisfy S = ABABA. However, if multiple pairs of A and B satisfy the condition, output the one with the smallest | AB |. If S is not a Mikomi string, output "mitomerarenaiWA".

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to determine if a string S can be written in the form ABABA where A and B are non-empty strings
2. If it can, we output "Love AB!" with the pair (A,B) that has the smallest |AB| (smaller combined length)
3. If not, we output "mitomerarenaiWA"

## Key Insights

Since S = ABABA, the total length of S is 5k where k = |A| + |B|.

For S = ABABA to be valid:
- The length of S must be divisible by 5
- A and B must be non-empty
- The string must match the pattern ABABA

## Analysis of Pattern

If S = ABABA, then:
- Positions 0 to |A|-1: A
- Positions |A| to |A|+|B|-1: B  
- Positions |A|+|B| to 2|A|+2|B|-1: A
- Positions 2|A|+2|B| to 3|A|+3|B|-1: B
- Positions 3|A|+3|B| to 4|A|+4|B|-1: A

So for each possible split point, we need to check if the string matches this pattern.

## Algorithm

1. Check if length of S is divisible by 5. If not, return "mitomerarenaiWA"
2. Let k = length(S) // 5
3. For each possible value of |A| from 1 to k-1 (since A and B must be non-empty), and |B| = k - |A|, check if S matches ABABA pattern
4. Among all valid pairs, pick the one with smallest |AB| = |A| + |B|
5. Since we want smallest |AB|, we should iterate from smaller |A| to larger |A|, and return the first valid pair

Actually, since |AB| = |A| + |B| = k, and k is fixed for a given string length, we just need to find ANY valid pair (A,B) and that will have the minimum |AB| for that length.

Wait, let me reconsider: for a fixed length, |AB| = |A| + |B| = k, which is constant. So any valid pair will have the same |AB|. We just need to find one valid pair.

But actually, not quite - if the length isn't divisible by 5, no solution. If it is divisible by 5, then |AB| = length/5, which is fixed.

So we just need to find if there exists any valid decomposition.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Check if len(S) % 5 == 0
2. If not, return "mitomerarenaiWA"
3. Let total_len = len(S), and k = total_len // 5
4. For each possible length of A from 1 to k-1:
   - Let len_B = k - len_A
   - Check if S[0:len_A] == S[len_A:len_A+len_B] == S[2*len_A+2*len_B:2*len_A+2*len_B+len_A] == S[3*len_A+3*len_B:3*len_A+3*len_B+len_B]
   - Actually, we need to check S[0:len_A] == S[2*len_A:2*len_A+len_A] == S[4*len_A:4*len_A+len_A] for A parts
   - And S[len_A:len_A+len_B] == S[2*len_A+len_B:2*len_A+2*len_B] == S[3*len_A+3*len_B:3*len_A+3*len_B+len_B] for B parts

Let me formalize:
- A occurs at positions: [0, len_A-1], [2*len_A, 2*len_A+len_A-1], [4*len_A, 4*len_A+len_A-1]
- B occurs at positions: [len_A, len_A+len_B-1], [2*len_A+len_B, 2*len_A+2*len_B-1], [3*len_A+3*len_B, 3*len_A+3*len_B+len_B-1]

## Implementation

```python
S = input().strip()

n = len(S)

# Check if length is divisible by 5
if n % 5 != 0:
    print("mitomerarenaiWA")
else:
    k = n // 5  # k = |A| + |B|
    
    found = False
    # Try all possible lengths for A from 1 to k-1
    # Since we want smallest |AB|, and |AB| = k is fixed, we just need to find one valid pair
    for len_a in range(1, k):
        len_b = k - len_a
        
        # Check if S = ABABA
        # A appears at positions: [0, len_a-1], [2*len_a, 2*len_a+len_a-1], [4*len_a, 4*len_a+len_a-1]
        # B appears at positions: [len_a, len_a+len_b-1], [2*len_a+len_b, 2*len_a+2*len_b-1], [3*len_a+3*len_b, 3*len_a+3*len_b+len_b-1]
        
        # Check A parts
        if (S[:len_a] != S[2*len_a:2*len_a+len_a] or 
            S[:len_a] != S[4*len_a:4*len_a+len_a]):
            continue
            
        # Check B parts
        if (S[len_a:len_a+len_b] != S[2*len_a+len_b:2*len_a+2*len_b] or 
            S[len_a:len_a+len_b] != S[3*len_a+3*len_b:3*len_a+3*len_b+len_b]):
            continue
            
        # All checks passed
        print(f"Love {S[:len_a]}!")
        found = True
        break
        
    if not found:
        print("mitomerarenaiWA")

Let me reconsider the pattern ABABA:
- A appears at positions 0 to |A|-1
- B appears at positions |A| to |A|+|B|-1  
- A appears at positions |A|+|B| to |A|+|B|+|A|-1
- B appears at positions |A|+|B|+|A| to |A|+|B|+|A|+|B|-1
- A appears at positions |A|+|B|+|A|+|B| to |A|+|B|+|A|+|B|+|A|-1

So the positions are:
- A: [0, |A|-1], [|A|+|B|, |A|+|B|+|A|-1], [|A|+|B|+|A|+|B|, |A|+|B|+|A|+|B|+|A|-1]
- B: [|A|, |A|+|B|-1], [|A|+|B|+|A|, |A|+|B|+|A|+|B|-1]

The total length is:
|A| + |B| + |A| + |B| + |A| = 3|A| + 2|B|

So we need 3|A| + 2|B| = n.

Also |A| >= 1, |B| >= 1.

Given |A| + |B| = k, we get 3|A| + 2(k - |A|) = |A| + 2k = n.

So |A| = n - 2k.

Since |A| must be >= 1, we need n - 2k >= 1, so n > 2k.

Also k must be such that n = 3|A| + 2|B|.

Given the total length n = 3|A| + 2|B|, and |A|, |B| >= 1.

I think there's a discrepancy. Let me assume the pattern is correct and rework.

For S = ABABA of length n, we have:
- A: length = a
- B: length = b
- Total length: 3a + 2b = n
- a >= 1, b >= 1

For each possible a (from 1 to n-2), b = (n - 3a) / 2, and b must be a positive integer.

So we need n - 3a > 0 and (n - 3a) % 2 == 0.

Since we want smallest |AB| = a + b, and b = (n - 3a) / 2, then |AB| = a + (n - 3a)/2 = (n - a)/2.

To minimize |AB|, we need to minimize (n - a)/2, which means maximizing a.

But since we want the pair with smallest |AB|, and |AB| = (n - a)/2, we want to minimize (n - a)/2, so we want to maximize a.

But a must satisfy the constraints: a >= 1, b >= 1, and (n - 3a) % 2 == 0.

So the maximum possible a is limited by b >= 1: n - 3a >= 2, so a <= (n-2)/3.

For maximum a, try a from floor((n-2)/3) down to 1.

```python
S = input().strip()

n = len(S)

# We need 3a + 2b = n, where a >= 1, b >= 1
# So b = (n - 3a) / 2 must be a positive integer
# Therefore: n - 3a >= 2 and (n - 3a) % 2 == 0

found = False
best_a = -1
best_b = -1

# Try all possible values of a from max possible down to 1 to find the one with max a (smallest |AB|)
max_a = (n - 2) // 3  # because n - 3a >= 2

for a in range(max_a, 0, -1):
    remainder = n - 3 * a
    if remainder >= 2 and remainder % 2 == 0:
        b = remainder // 2
        # Check if the string matches ABABA pattern
        # A: positions 0 to a-1
        # B: positions a to a+b-1
        # A: positions a+b to a+b+a-1
        # B: positions a+b+a to a+b+a+b-1
        # A: positions 2*(a+b) to 2*(a+b)+a-1
        
        # Check A segments
        if (S[:a] != S[a+b:a+b+a] or 
            S[:a] != S[2*(a+b):2*(a+b)+a]):
            continue
            
        # Check B segments
        if (S[a:a+b] != S[a+b+a:a+b+a+b] or 
            S[a:a+b] != S[2*(a+b)+a:2*(a+b)+a+b]):
            continue
            
        # Found valid pair
        print(f"Love {S[:a]}!")
        found = True
        best_a = a
        best_b = b
        break

if not found:
    print("mitomerarenaiWA")

Model Response

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to determine if a string S can be written in the form ABABA where A and B are non-empty strings
2. If it can, we output "Love AB!" with the smallest possible |AB| (length of AB)
3. If it cannot, we output "mitomerarenaiWA"

## Key Insights

1. The string must have length 5k for some k ≥ 1 (since ABABA has 5 segments)
2. For a string of length n = 5k, we need to find A and B such that:
   - A has length i (i ≥ 1)
   - B has length j (j ≥ 1)
   - i + j = k (since ABABA has 5 parts: A, B, A, B, A)
   - Total length = 3i + 2j = 5k

3. Actually, if A has length i and B has length j, then:
   - First A: length i
   - First B: length j  
   - Second A: length i
   - Second B: length j
   - Third A: length i
   - Total length = 3i + 2j

4. So we need 3i + 2j = n, where n = |S|

5. Since we want the smallest |AB| = i + j, we should try to minimize i + j subject to 3i + 2j = n and i ≥ 1, j ≥ 1.

## Mathematical Analysis

We have: 3i + 2j = n

We want to minimize: i + j

Let's express j in terms of i: j = (n - 3i)/2

For j to be positive: (n - 3i)/2 > 0, so n - 3i > 0, so i < n/3

Also, i ≥ 1 and i must make (n - 3i) even.

The value of i + j = i + (n - 3i)/2 = (2i + n - 3i)/2 = (n - i)/2

To minimize (n - i)/2, we need to maximize i, but i < n/3.

So we should try the largest possible i such that:
1. i < n/3
2. (n - 3i) is even and positive
3. i ≥ 1

## Algorithm Steps

1. Check if n is divisible by 5? Actually, no - n can be any length where 3i + 2j = n has solutions
2. For each possible i (from min to max), check if 3i + 2j = n has valid solutions
3. Among valid solutions, pick the one with maximum i (to minimize i+j)

Actually, let me reconsider the constraint:
- The string has length n
- We need 3i + 2j = n
- i ≥ 1, j ≥ 1
- j = (n - 3i)/2 must be a positive integer

So: n - 3i > 0, and n - 3i must be even, and (n - 3i)/2 ≥ 1

## Implementation

```python
s = input().strip()
n = len(s)

# Check if we can write s as ABABA
# We need 3i + 2j = n where i >= 1, j >= 1
# So j = (n - 3i)/2 >= 1, therefore n - 3i >= 2
# So i <= (n - 2)/3
# And i >= 1

found = False
result = ""

# Try all possible i values
# i must satisfy: 1 <= i <= (n-2)//3
max_i = (n - 2) // 3

for i in range(1, max_i + 1):
    remainder = n - 3 * i
    if remainder >= 2 and remainder % 2 == 0:
        j = remainder // 2
        # We found a valid decomposition
        # Check if s[0:i] == s[i:i+j] == s[2*i:2*i+j] == s[3*i:3*i+j]
        a = s[:i]
        b = s[i:i+j]
        
        # Verify the entire string matches ABABA
        expected = a + b + a + b + a
        if expected == s:
            # We found a valid solution
            # Since we're iterating from smallest i to largest, 
            # the last valid one will have maximum i, so minimum i+j
            result = f"Love {a}!"
            found = True
            break

if found:
    print(result)
else:
    print("mitomerarenaiWA")
```

Wait, I need to be more careful. I want the one with smallest |AB| = i + j.

Since i + j = i + (n-3i)/2 = (n - i)/2, to minimize i + j, I need to maximize i.

So I should iterate i from max_i down to 1, and take the first valid solution.

F: Miko Mi String-

story

Mikko Mikkomi ~! Everyone's idol, Miko Miko Tazawa! Today ~, with Mikoto ~, practice the string algorithm, let's do it ☆

Miko's special ~~ character making ~~ The slogan "MikoMikomi" becomes "MikoMikoMi" in Roman letters! In other words, if A = “Mi” and B = “Ko”, you can write in the form of ABABA! In this way, a character string that can be decomposed into the form of ABABA by properly determining A and B is called "Mikomi character string"! Miko is a popular person for everyone to become the name of a character string!

In order for everyone to use the Mikomi character string, I decided to make a program to judge whether the given character string is the Mikomi character string, but I can't write a program longer than Miko and FizzBuzz. !! So ~, for Miko ~, I want you to write a program that judges Mikomi character strings ☆

...... You said it's cold now! ??

problem

A character string S consisting of uppercase and lowercase letters is given. Here, if there are two non-empty strings A and B that can be written as S = ABABA, then S is said to be a "Mikomi string". At this time, uppercase and lowercase letters of the alphabet shall be distinguished as different characters. Create a program that determines whether a given string is a string.

Input format

The input consists of only one line including the character string S. It can be assumed that S satisfies the following conditions.

* 1 ≤ | S | ≤ 10 ^ 6. However, | S | represents the length of the character string S.
* S consists only of uppercase or lowercase alphabets.



Since the input may be very large, it is recommended to use a high-speed function to receive the input.

Output format

If S is a character string, output "Love AB!" For A and B that satisfy S = ABABA. However, if multiple pairs of A and B satisfy the condition, output the one with the smallest | AB |. If S is not a Mikomi string, output "mitomerarenaiWA".

NicoNicoNi

Output example 1


Love Nico!

mitomerarenaiWA

Output example 3


Love Live!

AizunyanPeroPero

Output example 4


mitomerarenaiWA

AAAAAAAAAAAAAA

Output example 5


Love AAAAA!

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to determine if a string S can be written in the form ABABA where A and B are non-empty strings
2. If it can, we output "Love AB!" with the pair (A,B) that has the smallest |AB| (length of A+B)
3. If it cannot, we output "mitomerarenaiWA"

## Key Insights

The string must be of the form ABABA, which means:
- Length of S must be 5k where k ≥ 1 (since ABABA has 5 segments)
- We can try all possible ways to split the string into ABABA pattern
- For a valid split, the first 3 segments (A+B+A) must match the last 3 segments (B+A+B) when comparing positions

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Check if length is valid**: The length must be divisible by 5, and at least 5
2. **Try all possible positions**: For each possible length of A (from 1 to n//5), and B (from 1 to remaining parts), check if the pattern holds
3. **Verify the pattern**: For ABABA pattern, we need:
   - S[0:len(A)] = S[2*len(A):3*len(A)]
   - S[len(A):len(A)+len(B)] = S[3*len(A)+len(B):4*len(A)+len(B)]
   - S[2*len(A):3*len(A)] = S[4*len(A):5*len(A)]
4. **Find the minimal AB**: Since we want the smallest |AB|, we try smaller values first

## Implementation Strategy

For length n, we need n % 5 == 0 and n ≥ 5.
Let k = n // 5 (number of segments).

We need to find A and B such that:
- A has length i, B has length j
- For ABABA pattern:
  - A = S[0:i]
  - B = S[i:i+j]
  - A = S[2*i:2*i+i] = S[2*i:3*i]
  - B = S[2*i+i:2*i+i+j] = S[3*i:3*i+j]
  - A = S[4*i:5*i] = S[4*i:5*i]

So we try all possible i (length of A) and j (length of B) such that:
- i ≥ 1 and j ≥ 1
- 4*i + j ≤ n (last segment ends at position 5*i + j, which should be n)

## Code Implementation

```python
s = input().strip()
n = len(s)

# Length must be divisible by 5 and at least 5
if n < 5 or n % 5 != 0:
    print("mitomerarenaiWA")
else:
    # n // 5 is the number of segments in ABABA pattern
    k = n // 5
    
    # Try all possible lengths for A (i) and B (j)
    # A can be from 1 to n//2 (since A appears 3 times and B appears 2 times)
    # We want minimal |AB| = i + j, so we iterate from small values
    
    found = False
    result_a = ""
    result_b = ""
    
    # Try all possible lengths for A
    for i in range(1, n//2 + 1):
        # For each A length, try all possible lengths for B
        # B length j must satisfy: 3*i + j <= n (last B segment ends at 3*i + j)
        # and 4*i + j <= n (last A segment ends at 4*i + j)
        
        # B can be from 1 to n - 3*i (so that 3*i + j <= n)
        for j in range(1, n - 3*i + 1):
            # Check if the pattern ABABA holds
            # ABABA = A + B + A + B + A
            
            # Check positions 0 to i: A
            if s[:i] != s[2*i:3*i]:
                continue
                
            # Check positions i to i+j: B
            if s[i:i+j] != s[3*i:3*i+j]:
                continue
                
            # Check positions 3*i to 4*i: A (should match the last A)
            if s[3*i:4*i] != s[0:i]:
                continue
                
            # Check positions 4*i to 5*i: A (should match the first A)
            if s[4*i:5*i] != s[0:i]:
                continue
            
            # If we reach here, we found a valid ABABA pattern
            # The minimal AB length is i + j since we're iterating from small i and j
            result_a = s[:i]
            result_b = s[i:i+j]
            found = True
            break
        
        if found:
            break
    
    if found:
        print(f"Love {result_a}{result_b}!")
    else:
        print("mitomerarenaiWA")
```

Wait, I see a bug in my logic. Let me reconsider the pattern:

For ABABA:
- A = S[0:i]
- B = S[i:i+j]
- A = S[2*i:3*i] 
- B = S[3*i:4*i]
- A = S[4*i:5*i]

So the conditions are:
1. S[0:i] == S[2*i:3*i]
2. S[i:i+j] == S[3*i:4*i]
3. S[2*i:3*i] == S[4*i:5*i]

Let me fix the code:

```python
s = input().strip()
n = len(s)

# Length must be divisible by 5 and at least 5
if n < 5 or n % 5 != 0:
    print("mitomerarenaiWA")
else:
    # Try all possible lengths for A (i) and B (j)
    found = False
    result_a = ""
    result_b = ""
    
    # A length can be from 1 to (n//5 * 3) - some bounds
    # We want minimal |AB|, so we try smaller values first
    for i in range(1, n // 3 + 1):  # i can't be too large since 2*i <= n - i (for A+B+A)
        # B length j must satisfy: 3*i + j <= n
        for j in range(1, n - 3*i + 1):
            # Check if ABABA pattern holds
            # A should be first and third segments
            if s[:i] != s[2*i:3*i]:
                continue
                
            # B should be second and fourth segments
            if s[i:i+j] != s[3*i:4*i]:
                continue
                
            # The third A should match the last A
            if s[2*i:3*i] != s[4*i:5*i]:
                continue
            
            # If we reach here, we have a valid pattern
            result_a = s[:i]
            result_b = s[i:i+j]
            found = True
            break
        
        if found:
            break
    
    if found:
        print(f"Love {result_a}{result_b}!")
    else:
        print("mitomerarenaiWA")

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have 4 types of tickets with different prices:
   - S seat (type 1): 6000 yen
   - A seat (type 2): 4000 yen
   - B seat (type 3): 3000 yen
   - C seat (type 4): 2000 yen

2. We're given 4 lines of input, each containing a ticket type (1-4) and the number of tickets sold for that type.

3. The ticket types are not necessarily given in order (S, A, B, C), so we need to map each type to its price.

4. For each line, we need to calculate the sales amount (price × quantity) and output it.

## Step-by-step Solution

### Step 1: Define the price mapping
Create a dictionary or mapping that associates each ticket type with its price:
- Type 1 → 6000 yen
- Type 2 → 4000 yen
- Type 3 → 3000 yen
- Type 4 → 2000 yen

### Step 2: Process each input line
For each line:
- Read the ticket type (t) and quantity (n)
- Look up the price for that ticket type
- Calculate sales amount = price × quantity
- Output the result

### Step 3: Verify with the example
Input:
```
3 10  → B seat, 10 tickets → 3000 × 10 = 30,000
1 4   → S seat, 4 tickets → 6000 × 4 = 24,000
4 1   → C seat, 1 ticket → 2000 × 1 = 2,000
2 5   → A seat, 5 tickets → 4000 × 5 = 20,000
```

Expected output:
```
30000
24000
2000
20000
```

This matches the expected output, so the solution is correct.

## Code Implementation

```python
# Define price mapping for each ticket type
price_map = {
    1: 6000,  # S seat
    2: 4000,  # A seat
    3: 3000,  # B seat
    4: 2000   # C seat
}

# Process each line of input
for _ in range(4):
    line = input().split()
    ticket_type = int(line[0])
    quantity = int(line[1])
    
    # Calculate sales amount
    sales_amount = price_map[ticket_type] * quantity
    
    # Output the result
    print(sales_amount)
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We have 4 types of tickets with different prices:
   - S seat (type 1): 6000 yen
   - A seat (type 2): 4000 yen
   - B seat (type 3): 3000 yen
   - C seat (type 4): 2000 yen

3. The ticket types don't appear in any particular order (not necessarily S, A, B, C).

4. For each line, we need to calculate the sales amount (price per ticket × number of tickets) and output it.

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Identify the price mapping
- Type 1 (S seat) = 6000 yen
- Type 2 (A seat) = 4000 yen  
- Type 3 (B seat) = 3000 yen
- Type 4 (C seat) = 2000 yen

### Step 2: Process each input line
For each line with format "type number", I need to:
1. Extract the ticket type and quantity

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Today is the ticket release date for Aizu Entertainment's recommended idol group "Akabeko & Koboushi". There are four types of tickets:

S seat 6000 yen
A seat 4000 yen
B seat 3000 yen
C seat 2000 yen

You, the sales manager, are excitedly waiting for the launch. Finally on sale. It's selling very well!

Shortly after the launch, I received a table summarizing the orders up to that point. Each row of the table shows the type and number of tickets sold so far. However, the ticket types do not always appear in the order of S, A, B, C. Create a program to find the sales amount for each row in this table.



input

Input data is given in the following format.


t1 n1
t2 n2
t3 n3
t4 n4


The input consists of 4 lines. Line i is given the integer ti (1 ≤ ti ≤ 4) for the ticket type and the integer ni (0 ≤ ni ≤ 10000) for the number of tickets. The integers 1, 2, 3, and 4 representing the ticket types represent S seats, A seats, B seats, and C seats, respectively. Numbers from 1 to 4 always appear once as values ​​for t1, t2, t3, and t4, but they are not always given in the order of 1, 2, 3, 4.

output

Output the sales amount for each line.

Example

Input

3 10
1 4
4 1
2 5


Output

30000
24000
2000
20000

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have 4 types of tickets with different prices:
   - S seat (type 1): 6000 yen
   - A seat (type 2): 4000 yen
   - B seat (type 3): 3000 yen
   - C seat (type 4): 2000 yen

2. We're given 4 lines of input, each containing a ticket type (1-4) and the number of tickets sold.

3. The ticket types don't appear in order (1,2,3,4), so we need to map each type to its price and calculate the sales amount for each line.

4. For each line, we need to output the total sales amount (price × quantity).

## Step-by-step Solution

### Step 1: Create a price mapping
Map each ticket type to its corresponding price:
- Type 1 (S seat) → 6000 yen
- Type 2 (A seat) → 4000 yen
- Type 3 (B seat) → 3000 yen
- Type 4 (C seat) → 2000 yen

### Step 2: Process each line
For each line with format "type quantity":
- Look up the price for that type
- Multiply price by quantity
- Output the result

### Step 3: Implementation

```python
# Read input and process each line
# Create a price dictionary for ticket types
price_map = {
    1: 6000,  # S seat
    2: 4000,  # A seat
    3: 3000,  # B seat
    4: 2000   # C seat
}

# Process each of the 4 lines
for _ in range(4):
    line = input().split()
    ticket_type = int(line[0])
    quantity = int(line[1])
    
    # Calculate sales amount for this line
    sales_amount = price_map[ticket_type] * quantity
    
    # Output the result
    print(sales_amount)
```

## Verification with Example

Input:
- 3 10 → B seat: 3000 × 10 = 30000 ✓
- 1 4 → S seat: 6000 × 4 = 24000 ✓
- 4 1 → C seat: 2000 × 1 = 2000 ✓
- 2 5 → A seat: 4000 × 5 = 20000 ✓

Output matches expected: 30000, 24000, 2000, 20000

The solution is correct!

    print(sales_amount)

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have 4 types of tickets with different prices:
   - S seat (type 1): 6000 yen
   - A seat (type 2): 4000 yen
   - B seat (type 3): 3000 yen
   - C seat (type 4): 2000 yen

2. We're given 4 lines of input, each containing:
   - Ticket type (1-4) 
   - Number of tickets sold

3. We need to calculate the sales amount for each line (price × quantity)

4. The ticket types don't appear in order, so we need to map each type to its price.

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Create a price mapping
Create a dictionary to map ticket types to their prices:
- Type 1 → 6000 yen
- Type 2 → 4000 yen  
- Type 3 → 3000 yen
- Type 4 → 2000 yen

### Step 2: Process each line
For each input line:
- Extract the ticket type and quantity
- Look up the price for that type
- Calculate total sales = price × quantity
- Output the result

### Step 3: Verify with example
Input:
- 3 10 → B seat, 10 tickets → 3000 × 10 = 30,000
- 1 4 → S seat, 4 tickets → 6000 × 4 = 24,000
- 4 1 → C seat, 1 ticket → 2000 × 1 = 2,000
- 2 5 → A seat, 5 tickets → 4000 × 5 = 20,000

This matches the expected output!

## Code Implementation

```python
# Create price mapping for each ticket type
price_map = {
    1: 6000,  # S seat
    2: 4000,  # A seat
    3: 3000,  # B seat
    4: 2000   # C seat
}

# Process each line of input
for _ in range(4):
    line = input().split()
    ticket_type = int(line[0])
    quantity = int(line[1])
    
    # Calculate sales amount
    sales_amount = price_map[ticket_type] * quantity
    
    # Output the result
    print(sales_amount)
```

The solution is straightforward:
1. We create a dictionary that maps each ticket type (1-4) to its price
2. For each of the 4 input lines, we parse the ticket type and quantity
3. We look up the price for the ticket type and multiply by the quantity
4. We print the sales amount for each line

Time complexity: O(1) - constant time for each input line
Space complexity: O(1) - we only store the price mapping with fixed size

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have 4 types of tickets with different prices:
   - S seat (type 1): 6000 yen
   - A seat (type 2): 4000 yen  
   - B seat (type 3): 3000 yen
   - C seat (type 4): 2000 yen

2. We're given 4 lines of input, each containing a ticket type (1-4) and the number of tickets sold for that type.

3. The ticket types aren't necessarily given in order (1, 2, 3, 4), so we need to map each type to its price.

4. For each line, we need to calculate the sales amount by multiplying the ticket type's price by the number of tickets sold.

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Define the price mapping
Create a dictionary or mapping from ticket type to price:
- Type 1 (S) → 6000 yen
- Type 2 (A) → 4000 yen
- Type 3 (B) → 3000 yen
- Type 4 (C) → 2000 yen

### Step 2: Process each input line
For each line, extract the ticket type and number of tickets, then calculate the sales amount.

### Step 3: Output the results
Print each sales amount on a separate line.

## Implementation

```python
# Define price mapping for each ticket type
ticket_prices = {
    1: 6000,  # S seat
    2: 4000,  # A seat
    3: 3000,  # B seat
    4: 2000   # C seat
}

# Read 4 lines of input
for _ in range(4):
    line = input().split()
    ticket_type = int(line[0])
    number_of_tickets = int(line[1])
    
    # Calculate sales amount
    sales_amount = ticket_prices[ticket_type] * number_of_tickets
    
    # Output the sales amount
    print(sales_amount)
```

## Verification with Example

Input:
- 3 10 → Type 3 (B seat) × 10 = 3000 × 10 = 30000 ✓
- 1 4 → Type 1 (S seat) × 4 = 6000 × 4 = 24000 ✓
- 4 1 → Type 4 (C seat) × 1 = 2000 × 1 = 2000 ✓
- 2 5 → Type 2 (A seat) × 5 = 4000 × 5 = 20000 ✓

Output matches the expected result perfectly.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have 4 types of tickets with different prices:
   - S seat (type 1): 6000 yen
   - A seat (type 2): 4000 yen
   - B seat (type 3): 3000 yen
   - C seat (type 4): 2000 yen

2. We receive 4 lines of input, each containing a ticket type (1-4) and the number of tickets sold.

3. The ticket types don't appear in order (S, A, B, C), so we need to map each type to its price and calculate the total sales for each row.

4. For each row, we need to output the sales amount (price × number of tickets).

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Define the price mapping**: Create a dictionary or list that maps ticket type to price
   - Type 1 → 6000 yen
   - Type 2 → 4000 yen  
   - Type 3 → 3000 yen
   - Type 4 → 2000 yen

2. **Process each line**: For each input line, extract the ticket type and number of tickets.

3. **Calculate sales**: Multiply the number of tickets by the price of that ticket type.

4. **Output the result**: Print the sales amount for each line.

## Implementation

```python
# Define price mapping for ticket types
# Type 1: S seat = 6000 yen
# Type 2: A seat = 4000 yen
# Type 3: B seat = 3000 yen
# Type 4: C seat = 2000 yen

price_map = {
    1: 6000,
    2: 4000,
    3: 3000,
    4: 2000
}

# Read 4 lines of input
for _ in range(4):
    line = input().split()
    ticket_type = int(line[0])
    number_of_tickets = int(line[1])
    
    # Calculate sales amount for this row
    sales_amount = price_map[ticket_type] * number_of_tickets
    
    # Output the sales amount
    print(sales_amount)
```

## Verification with Example

Input:
- 3 10: Type 3 (B seat) × 10 = 3000 × 10 = 30000 ✓
- 1 4: Type 1 (S seat) × 4 = 6000 × 4 = 24000 ✓
- 4 1: Type 4 (C seat) × 1 = 2000 × 1 = 2000 ✓
- 2 5: Type 2 (A seat) × 5 = 4000 × 5 = 20000 ✓

The output matches the expected result.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have 4 types of tickets with different prices:
   - S seat (type 1): 6000 yen
   - A seat (type 2): 4000 yen
   - B seat (type 3): 3000 yen
   - C seat (type 4): 2000 yen

2. We're given 4 lines of input, each containing a ticket type (1-4) and the number of tickets sold for that type.

3. The ticket types don't appear in order (1,2,3,4), so we need to map each type to its price and calculate the sales amount for each row.

4. For each input line, we need to calculate: (number of tickets) × (price of that ticket type)

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Map ticket types to prices**: Create a dictionary to map each ticket type (1-4) to its corresponding price.
   - Type 1 → 6000 yen (S seat)
   - Type 2 → 4000 yen (A seat)
   - Type 3 → 3000 yen (B seat)
   - Type 4 → 2000 yen (C seat)

2. **Process each input line**: For each line, extract the ticket type and number of tickets, then calculate the sales amount.

3. **Output the result**: Print the sales amount for each line.

## Implementation

```python
# Read input and process each line
ticket_prices = {
    1: 6000,  # S seat
    2: 4000,  # A seat
    3: 3000,  # B seat
    4: 2000   # C seat
}

# Process each of the 4 lines
for _ in range(4):
    line = input().split()
    ticket_type = int(line[0])
    num_tickets = int(line[1])
    
    # Calculate sales amount for this row
    sales_amount = num_tickets * ticket_prices[ticket_type]
    print(sales_amount)
```

## Verification with Example

Input:
- "3 10": Type 3 (B seat) × 10 tickets = 10 × 3000 = 30,000 ✓
- "1 4": Type 1 (S seat) × 4 tickets = 4 × 6000 = 24,000 ✓
- "4 1": Type 4 (C seat) × 1 ticket = 1 × 2000 = 2,000 ✓
- "2 5": Type 2 (A seat) × 5 tickets = 5 × 4000 = 20,000 ✓
